Makes spilling their guts all the more poetic." Fable hummed, running a tongue over his teeth briefly. The promise earned a nod in return. "You'll get your bites if I have anything to say about it. Just a matter of when, not if." He said, handing Hazzel back his empty cup. The man took the hint, accepting it in one hand and raising his own to finish it with the other. The shade turned to more directly look at the pack digging in behind them. "Looks like they're making short work of the evidence... Us leavin before they're done ain't gonna spook em off completely, would it? I'd hate to leave anything important laying around." (edited)
Daarka 2/2/2026 5:40 PM
The fanged grin that cleaved the moondevil's face nearly in twain might not have been a kind or loving expression--but it was earnest, and the malice it carried wasn't for the thing they were grinning at, for once. And that was kind of like a heartfelt sentiment of kinship or affection. "Hm? Nah." They threw back the rest of their own coffee, setting the mug back down before leaning forward in a rearrangement of skin. "You travelin' with a diplomat," they lilted amusedly, taking their form that matched the shadows tearing bodies apart behind them. "Get situated to start headin' back." They, on the other hand, trotted forward to move closer to the other coyotes--who eyed them warily at first, noses twitching towards their sudden appearance as one of them. But even to the untrained eye, even to a layman who knew nothing about the behavior of coyotes--even canines at all--there was undeniably a warm welcome. Recognition, even. Not even a beat of resource guarding over the fresh meat, but a pleasant wave of delighted surprise as that pack, by all indication, caught up with an old friend. It would be easy to lose track of which one was the moondevil, the tawny pelts and wagging tails and occasional warbling vocalizations of excitement near impossible to distinguish from one another. The demon--already full--made no attempt towards their feast, but they didn't behave as if they were worried they would. Just because men had forgotten who the gods of the desert were didn't mean the desert had. Just because men had drawn the unrestrained ire of those desert gods didn't mean they weren't perfectly benevolent stewards of their intended flock. The coyote pack had no reason to fear or flinch from the god who had granted them this bounty. The coyote pack had no reason to be wary of the man in their midst; their god was here. The moondevil didn't dally long. By the time Hazzel had gathered his things and loot and conjured back his mount, the god-coyote had separated from the pack again to leave them to their ripping and tearing, giving a satisfied, full-body shake as they trotted back towards Hazzel. "You know... In some deeply indirect way, think I can understand how you find children anything other than stupid and annoying," they rattled, drawing back upward into their largest body as they glanced over their shoulder. "Them pups grow like weeds. Blink and you'll miss it. Spring litter nearly the size of the rest now." Evidently, the pack were no more strangers to the moondevil than they were to the pack. (edited)
Woozy 2/2/2026 5:59 PM
Hazzel nearly wished he could've watched more, the way they interacted together. He'd enjoyed his time well enough with their unusual companion, but it was something else to see them in their natural element, as it were. As it were, he cast them the occasional glimpse as he cleaned out the coffee cups and shucked them away with what remaining supplies he hadn't finished pocketing. The rest of the food he left where it was; it wasn't nearly as much or anything like the corpses the pack was digging into, but it was his own little snack-treat to leave em. If not them, at least someone in the desert would appreciate it. The shade had disappeared at some point between the mingling and the summoning. With the night long, it was hard to tell where his attention was, too. Unlike Hazzel he had no need to tuck anything into his nonexistent pockets, nor did he need to carve the ritual circle to summon a mount. Gave him time instead to observe the coyotes in an almost vicarious indulgence of the familiarity they had together. The shadows said nothing of the exchange, only watching from their dual distance and closeness under trotting feet and wagging tails, and the edges of ruined wagons. Hazzel looked over as they came back over, brows furrowing only briefly before he caught their meaning. He let out a soft laugh, nodding, and glanced back towards the pack again for one final look. "They grow up quick, don't they? Born and gone, like fireflies blinking in and out at night. How long you've lived... shit, you probably know that even better than I do." A nudge to Sue's sides sent her moving, though he lingered a bit in that slow methodic plodding on the way out of camp. "My son's grown up now, has a son of his own now too. I still remember how he looked when he was just born though. How little he weighed. Crazy, seeing the way age has started creeping into him too."
Daarka 2/2/2026 6:06 PM
The moondevil fell into step alongside the old mare, even though each stride of theirs threatened to leave her in the very slow dust. But they turned their head to give Hazzel a strange look; even without a humanoid face to emote it properly, it was strong enough to accurately convey a stroke of bewilderment. “You weren’t lyin’ ‘bout grandbabies? When the fuck did you procreate?”
Woozy 2/2/2026 6:21 PM
Hazzel barked out a laugh at that. "No, I wasn't lyin." He said with a wink before settling back into his chair to properly think about it. His eyes went skyward for a moment. "Had to have been about 50 years ago or so by now. Not too long after I met Fable. Met a woman I liked.. didn't hurt that Fable liked her well enough too. I'm sure you know how the rest of that goes." He let out a wistful sigh. "I still visit from time to time. Make sure they're keeping right, and making trouble go away if they ain't. Best as I can, anyways. My grandson now, he's got a little wicked streak. Good strength.. think his dad's been having him help out with the local blacksmith. Try and put that strength to productive use. Only time'll tell if that works out or not."
Daarka 2/2/2026 6:52 PM
The way the demon gawked implied that they not only assumed the other mentions had been part of the countless tall tales and fabrications that came out of the man’s mouth, but also that they hadn’t for one single second considered that Hazzel was a person with human connections and relationships and not just an anomaly that existed in a vacuum. After all, they themselves were an anomaly that existed in a vacuum. They weren’t hardwired to make connections other than the ones they arrived with. They couldn’t relate to anything but their immediate family. And they realized, in that beat, the flaws in their capacity to relate; the more they saw Hazzel as something like an equal, the less they saw him as a person. Being reminded he very much was a person was more jarring than they expected. But they shut their mouth from where their jaw had gone a bit slack. Blinking once as they grappled with the idea that there were little spawns of Hazzel adrift somewhere out there. People who they still could not relate to, but were also not quite in the same category as the other human prey they looked down upon. And that was, simply, too many categorizations to comfortably hold at once. Too many shades of nuance to paint their world in for the time being. They were pulled from those Brand New Thoughts by the distant call of an owl, drifting sleepily through the dark. Their ears turned to catch the sound, and whatever they heard in it seemed to remind them they were on a time crunch here. They had no way to give him a genuine reply that also concisely wrapped the topic up. With another shake-out of their pelt—more dramatic than before with their increased size—they reoriented their thoughts towards the run back to Jubilation. “Time for that later,” they declared, turning to land a nip on the mare’s flank. They didn’t know (or care) if the conjured horse could be harmed, but the bite did little more than a spur would—the moondevil turning and launching forward immediately after. The run back was just as long as the first, and they had the exhaustion of that first length and the massacre that ensued under their belts to make it that bit more tedious—but it was counterbalanced by the absence of the sun straining overhead, the breeze dragging over their racing forms so cool it was nearly too cold. Hours later, they would be welcomed back to the outskirts of Jubilation by the unapologetically gorgeous glow of a cresting sunrise and the gentle song of mourning doves calling back and forth across the desert. The moondevil dropped from their breakneck sprint to ooze back to a trot, letting their shape collapse down to a more conventional coyote, lest any of Jubilation’s eyes have strayed too far from town to glimpse a monster. They were panting heavily again, but still moving steadily forward, even the grueling test of endurance not enough to leave them limping or sagging on their feet. “Town,” they wheezed between breaths, “Can get you some breakfast. Show our faces. Then sleep.”
Woozy 2/2/2026 7:28 PM
Though Hazzel could tell the concept had eluded something of the moondevil's understanding, he couldn't fully quite understand just what about it had. To him, his going forth and having children was of little difference than the coyote's the demon had been consorting with moments ago. But then, he wasn't a demon. Men, he supposed dryly, had a way of complicating things regardless of subject. The topic shifted though, and though Sue seemed content to plod forward with the demon at her side, the nip to her haunches was enough to set her eyes wide and kickstart the fire inside her just as surely as it would any horse. Hazzel grit his teeth and leaned in, holding onto the reins for dear life. Briefly he wondered if he perhaps shouldn't have eaten as much as he did before such a run. It was hard to think on the run back. It was simultaneously the only thing Hazzel could do. It didn't take long for the motions of riding settled into his body, aches flaring anew from the previous demand that only grew stronger as the night wore on. The adrenaline could only carry him so far, and the only thing that kept him from nodding off on the run was the breakneck speed at which they tackled their task with. The slow bloom of sunrise brought with it relief, a gentle reward for all their hard work through the night. As the moondevil fell to a trot he too slowed down. Sue wheezed beneath him, her speed dropping rapidly to a trot, briefly, and then back to her other preferred speed of not. Hazzel slumped against his saddle and buried his face briefly in the back of the mare's neck, reaching down to pat her. "That'll do, girl." He wheezed. He pulled up from that position (grunting with a hiss as he did) at their voice and nodded. They still had appearances to make. He sucked in a steadying breath and finally beckoned the mare to stop, dismounting to walk the rest of the way into town on his own feet. By the time he'd hit the ground, he'd sharpened up his appearance in slight ways; the bags of his eyes had disappeared, his clothes were tidy and free of any splotches of stray blood he might've missed cleaning at night, and his skin was flush and warm and definitely not flagging from the late night. Ignoring the aches screaming across his body, he set out towards town at his usual pace. "Not even that hungry at this point," He grimaced under his breath. His canteen pulled free from his hips to raise, and he took a greedy gulp of water. "Another coffee though. That I could go for."
Daarka 2/2/2026 7:46 PM
As Hazzel halted to dismount, the moondevil came to a stop as well, rising back to a bipedal height with a bit more effort than usual—and a long, aching groan. They were no old man in a saddle, their body was meant for such activity—but it was still quite a lot in close succession. Fortunately, their posture as a “human” had always been a slouching, swaying thing, so it wasn’t very apparent at all that their current slouching was one uniquely bought by over-exertion. Stretching their back out, they didn’t bother stifling a yawn, a hand lifting to halfassedly comb some debris from a cholla they had brushed when racing past from the tangles of their hair using their long claws. “Don’t matter what. Don’t even think we gotta make a show of walkin’ around.” They glanced over towards Hazzel directly. “Not if we’re efficient. You’d know better than me: which breakfast joint owned or operated by the loosest lips? One who’d be most vocal and forthcomin’ with havin’ witnessed us in their establishment this mornin’? We can plant our asses there a spell, then head to bed.”
Woozy 2/2/2026 8:26 PM
The question was a welcome distraction from his aches. "Oh, that's easy. That'd be... that'd be Molly Monday's. From what I hear, Missus Molly opened up the place just to make gettin rumors in her ears all the easier. Helps she's a damn good cook, too. Makes a mean stack of flapjacks 'n honey." With a destination in mind, Hazzel seemed to have just a smidge more enthusiasm nudging his old bones into town. Despite his aches, he moved easily through the folks starting their mornings, greeting them as they passed in perfect polite company. Once they got close enough through the streets to smell the breakfast cooking on the wind, it was just enough to urge him on further into her establishment. Molly Monday's was a small restaurant that made clever work fitting in all the tables it did inside and still have room for people to bustle on in. It was tight, but it worked, so long as you weren't afraid of saying "excuse me" every few minutes to squeeze past someone. It was busy enough, which meant that it took about two and a half "excuse me"s to squeeze through over towards a far corner, right on past a bunch of townsfolk who all got their own morning "hello"s and "well how you doin?"s. Didn't stop him from calling out one of the waitresses either, in a polite but loud enough voice to call over the dining and conversational din. "Mornin, Grace! You got any flapjacks still this morning?" One of the waitresses turned from her conversation with a cook to grin and wave the pair's way, a short and plump woman with rosy cheeks and crow's feet. "Mornin Mr. Hazzel! I'll get you some flapjacks, don't you worry none. Coffee?" "Yes please!" He called back, settling into his seat with a much relieved sigh.
Daarka 2/2/2026 8:55 PM
The moondevil found they rather appreciated letting Hazzel’s far more easily-produced cheer pave the way for them, as opposed to them putting in the extra effort to don that mask themselves. It wasn’t even really a mask for Hazzel. They were happy to mooch off its perks. But though they were not as loudly outgoing as Hazzel, they did put in the effort to have a pleasant look on their face, issuing greetings of canned pleasantries to those who greeted them (or, really, greeted Hazzel). Likewise, they were content to let Hazzel do the yelling once they’d slouched into the seat at the bustling breakfast joint, continuing to ride the coattails of his extroversion. They did pipe up as food was being ordered though. “Make that two stacks, if you don’t mind,” they rasped, somehow managing to do so in a way that carried at a serviceable volume despite their breathy voice. When that was squared, they slumped deeper into their seat with a sort of ill-postured comfort. “Knew it was best to ask you,” they noted wryly, gaze wandering the packed establishment. They comfortably sat in that ambient din a moment, before piping up again with one of the few topics of conversation they could entertain in such close quarters with strangers. “Think you said you still visit that babymomma of yours. Where she at?” A demon asking for the location of Hazzel’s loved ones would, under any other circumstances, be objectively alarming—but it was probably fine.
Woozy 2/2/2026 10:46 PM
"Two stacks, you got it sweetheart!" Grace called back over her shoulder to flash the demon a smile before returning her attention to the cook and a fresh slip for their order. Hazzel was perfectly still for a few long moments in his seat as he too seemed to slump a little. One would be forgiven to wonder if the old man had dozed off (especially when knowing what he'd been up to all night) though his companion's wry note earned a wide smile that spread slow and comfortably across his lips. It wasn't until they spoke again to pick back up roundabout their previous conversation that he opened his eyes and adjusted to sit up a bit more properly. "I do, yeah. Her name's Rhea. Send her and my son money when I can, help support em when I'm not around. Dammon, he's a good man. Takes care of her." He rubbed at his knuckles, smile tempering somewhat. "She ain't been too good in recent years, hardly remembers me some days. Last time I stuck around to try and help I just made things worse, so..." He shrugged lightly. There was genuine concern and some level of hurt about it evident in his expression. There was also a level of distance and what looked like acceptance at first glance that sung similar to those who made Necessary Plans for loved ones. The demon wasn't most people though, and beneath that was a layer of purposeful detachment. A level of understanding that it was inevitable, what was happening. He paused to thank Grace as she appeared long enough to pour the two a pair of coffees. He took a long sip, sighing in contentment that chased away all of those emotions until his expression seemed back to what was more familiar. "They're in a small town not too far north of Talmas, actually. Called Aldman Watch. Supposedly it used to be an old fort, but it's more of a trade stop between Talmas and Aedrenor these days. Lot of the wood down here comes from there, actually."

Woozy 2/7/2026 4:47 PM

Woozy 2/13/2026 12:08 AM

Even as the harp appeared in the space in front of him, his hands found their purchase on the smooth white wood. As he sat, a soft click emanated from the base of the harp. The whole harp seemed to shift and lean against Hazzel's shoulder for support, who bore it with similarly practiced ease. Fingers flirted briefly with the strings, adjusting the tuning of one until he was satisfied.
Daarka 2/13/2026 1:00 AM

And skills like that didn’t travel easily when they required very hard-to-transport equipment—so not-harpists Talmans remained. -# And ignorant-to-harps moondevils remained.
Woozy 2/13/2026 7:18 AM

Daarka 2/13/2026 12:41 PM
Music was a grating thing--just noise for noise's sake. It could be tolerated for the moments they encountered it, but they had never liked it. It wouldn't take much genius for Hazzel or Fable to guess at how little love they held for The Arts of mankind. And if the twist of their ears was any indication, they were braced for just such an experience. But further heckling did not make it past their teeth. They knew music from feverish taverns and stuffy chapels, and this was neither. Sure, there was something inevitably similar in the sound of harp strings to other stringed instruments, but it was distinctly unlike the clangor of a strummed guitar or the plinking twang of piano keys. It didn't aggressively vie for attention or position itself as reverent. The confident but soft-spoken melody was nearly jarring in how comparatively inoffensive it was. The melody itself set it apart, too. If the moondevil knew nothing else about Hazzel, they would have known with what he played now that he had spent considerable time outside of Talmas; there was a flavor to the music that just didn't exist locally. And as someone who had only ever exclusively been a local, that made it entirely brand new to the coyote. An ear had deviated from the preemptively flattened state they'd been in to turn towards Fable as the greater demon took shape and spoke his pride. They could understand his pride. They'd barely known this man half a moon cycle, and he had grown on them immensely. They hadn't yet figured out where jackalopes fell between categorizations of mortals versus things the moondevil was more predisposed to respect--but whatever the man had going on, mortal or not, was enough to intrigue, impress, and keep pace with not one, but now two demons (and of very different backgrounds, too). It was almost a shame that such things were better kept as closely-guarded secrets, because it was a kind of feat worth bragging about. But the moondevil did not find the words for a reply to Fable, just as they had not produced any heckling. It didn't take long for the second ear to lift out of its flattened twist, joining the first in loosely lifting to a relaxed forward angle as they gradually settled their chin onto their paws to listen, their gaze quietly captivated watching Hazzel's fingers move. Despite Hazzel's threat to double his noise output by speaking as he played, the coyote offered no conversation for the duration. Even when the song concluded, their muzzle remained soundly shut for a few moments, before their head eased up off their paws again. "... You could teach these rancid sonsuvbitches 'round here a thing or two," they finally commented in a dry rattle. (edited)
Woozy 2/16/2026 8:26 PM
There was a certain pride that came with completely sidestepping a heckling. Hazzel wasn't stupid; he could very easily see the way those ears flattened back in preparation for his noise-making. Despite his own threats to make more noise alongside the strings, when no further objections arose he allowed his attention to sink into the strings. His eyelids nearly fluttered closed at times, blinking open just long enough to quickly gauge the distance to the next string even as his hands were moving. One would be forgiven in assuming he could do the whole thing blinded if he needed to. As the song came to an end, his hands delicately pressed to the strings to silence them, for but a moment before starting another. As the coyote spoke up, he finally looked over, almost like he'd forgotten where he was, or who he was with. At their comment he huffed out a dry laugh, lips curling into something of a sneer at the idea. His gaze fixed back upon the strings again, picking up tempo. "These skills would be wasted on the people here. Every last one of them. I've been wandering for gods-know how many years, and I have yet to find a single person worth teaching it to this whole time." He paused speaking, focusing on a trill until the tempo slowed again, briefly. "It ain't just music. It's magic too. And none of them have the right spark for it."
Daarka 2/16/2026 8:50 PM
The second song proved to be no more offensive than the first had been--and the moondevil found that developing trend unexpectedly pleasant. They didn't respond immediately, transfixed into silence by the music for a few measures longer before they found their voice. "You say that like the conjurin' part ain't the magic you're referrin' to," they prompted simply, shifting their body to settle in more comfortably now that they were no longer contemplating evacuating the hut, hind legs stretching behind them in a lazy sploot. Woozy 2/16/2026 10:49 PM

Daarka 2/23/2026 3:53 PM

This was precisely as uncomfortable as it looked. Daarka 3/5/2026 7:35 PM
When the sun rose again, the work began—with seven days left until the blood moon. Every time the moondevil walked past the tavern, they felt their eyes on them. Never went back in, though. They didn’t have to tell those skulls they were coming for them. Didn’t have to reassure the bones it wouldn’t be much longer. Dogs kept dying. Cats, too. The coyote filled their belly in the desert; when they fell upon the Jubilation homesteads, it was for sport. It was to make increasing messes of things with names and collars and lovingly-built shelters. After a day or two, it stopped being things a coyote should have been capable of doing. The dog wasn’t just dead and disemboweled; its dripping, disembodied head was lodged atop a branch, which was impaled into the ground like an effigy. There was something preciously potent in the way a functionally mundane gesture could be something so deeply unsettling, could raise nightmarishly dark questions that had no answers. Doorways started getting painted in that spilled blood. Runes smeared onto the flaking wood, using that viscera like paint. Circles and sigils of surprising competence arranged, composed solely of innards. They were having fun. It started on one side of town. It’d put the immediate area on alert of some roaming, nocturnal monster—but that monster would always strike just outside of that area on alert. And that monster walked the streets in the broad daylight to hear as disquiet began to spread. Everyone assumed it a simple beast at first. Until the effigies. Until the sigils. Until the responsive measures folks started taking—double locking gates, keeping a shotgun by the door, laying out traps—weren’t stopping anything. The tragedy had spread like a virus and reached halfway through the town in scattered instances of seemingly unrelated but similarly abhorrent strokes of violence before it stopped being just the animals left in pieces. Someone’s little boy had never been taught not to follow coyotes. Someone’s little boy had such a fragile, soft little neck that split like the skin on a grape. Someone’s little boy didn’t even manage a whimper, and choked on his crushed throat between a blanket of shadows and a carpet of gravel and thorn. When that little boy didn’t come inside in time for supper, it took them quite a while to find him. Took them quite a while to think to check the stall in the barn out back where his soft and squishy little body had be snapped and bent to tangle into the bars of the cattle’s manger like an idly woven ribbon. Teeth marks on his neck—but not a bite of flesh removed. -# It always was the last place you looked. There was an exorcism on the house. The miserable family and shouting priest and his men didn’t notice the coyote off in the brush. Why would they? It had been specially crafted by the desert to melt seamlessly into her sands, all but invisible to the untrained eye. So it watched, unblinkingly. Amusedly. The land was cleansed of impure spirits. One of those spirits had fled into one of the cattle. The cattle was shot dead. It was fascinating theater. The priest sure projected from the diaphragm plenty enough that the coyote got every word. There was no beat to recover. No time for the news to spread the next day. As the sun set and the little boy’s house was cleansed, the coyote found a second house. Found the room of a little girl. One they had danced at before, who they saw would have followed them. They hadn’t taken her up on it that time, uncharacteristically. She was for later. She was for now. (edited)
7:35 PM
Little girls who thought themselves magical, who read stories of princesses that all the wild animals flocked to—they roused from their beds when little bobcats tapped on their windows. Woozy 3/5/2026 9:15 PM
Something about this particular stay in the desert had been drudging up a lot of old memories for Hazzel. It's not like he was the kind of man to ignore his memories, good or bad, no. He'd simply committed his life to one of crafting new ones through his eternal pilgrimage in these lands. There was a lot of land around. There was a lot of memories to be made. And yet, in the hushed hours of the day, inside diners and taverns and inns and under porches and canvas shades, the one that seemed to keep creeping back into mind recently was one of his father, after a long, trying night. Of all the things that could possibly disturb a man - horrors, gore, putting a beloved animal down after a stray trap had degloved a leg and turned into sepsis - nothin seemed to ruffle Hazzel more than telling a lie. Sure, all the former might've gotten under his skin and caused a whole lot of distress of varying degrees in the moment, but none of it ever stuck the way a lie stuck to his palms like a cold sweat. Course, he'd lost even the effects of that over the years. It served him well, here, as people brought in their hushed whispers and worries. Professional gossip that he was, there was nothing he liked more than bending an ear towards all those worries, soothing them over with a well intentioned coo and pat to the hand. There were only so many platitudes that one man could make though, and as the crimes stretched on and increased in creativity, he found platitudes less useful compared to matching their worry. It was a bad day when even the optimist started to worry. Pretend to worry, anyways. The only real worry Hazzel had was that there'd been no sign of the coins yet, which meant he'd lost his bet of one week. Hardly a worry, since it meant that was just one more thing to look forward to, but it did make his coinpurse all the lighter for it. Surely that was a worry for someone. He was drawn out of his thoughts at the oncoming evening rituals by the interruption, pausing his stride and lowering the hand poised to pull his tome free to enact those nightly rituals. The goad was curious, a promise of entertainment. As if the day's gossip wasn't enough. Interest piqued, his head tilted slightly after a moment, a slow smile spreading. "You got dinner plans then, I take it?"

Daarka 3/6/2026 2:32 PM
By now, the coyote had gotten delightedly used to how easily Hazzel kept pace with them. They had spent an eternity surrounded by kin so likeminded in almost all matters, that they had practically inhabited the same brain; there had almost never been a need for all the communicative fussing most individuals would have to do to cooperate towards a shared goal. Likeminded though they had been, they had each had their own strengths and weaknesses. They knew what each other was capable of, knew what each other's limits were. Knew the precise threshold where one would no longer be able to handle something on their own. They were individuals, but they were aligned and unified in ways the individualism of men made impossible. No questions. No double-checking. No guessing. No blindspots. Hazzel--nor even Fable--would ever think precisely like a coyote, so the moondevil was under no illusion they could recreate such synchronicity. But there was a certain satisfaction that kept being reinforced every time they threw something, and the old man didn't flinch or balk or shudder in his sensitivities, didn't argue or criticize or deviate, didn't let the pitch slip through his fingers and leave the demon picking up the mess--he just jumped and caught it effortlessly and without hesitation. With joy, even. Sometimes even did a little flip in the air. The moondevil had yet to catch him in a moment of incompetence, yet to find the limits of his constitution. So when he told them not to worry, they didn't. "Not so far from the house that she'll be too hard to find," the coyote replied to his question about placement. "I ain't plannin' to make noise, but don't want the neighbors catchin' wind, either. Just out where I can see her real good." When Hazzel gestured for them to stay put, they obligingly slowed to a halt, easing down after a moment onto their belly where they had a line of sight towards the back door. As they fell still in that position, even the strength of the moonlight wasn't enough to distinguish their outline against the sand and brush to an untrained eye at even a moderate distance--so they comfortably waited and watched. The shapechanging, of course, piqued their interest plenty, and they momentarily wondered if there were any other bodies hiding in there that they hadn't seen yet. It remained fortunate that they had the intelligence and self-restraint to not act on the base urges to shoot forward after the delightfully-moving, pale little prey thing and catch it by those antlers. But they didn't stop themselves from thinking about it a little. The song was enough to break their perfect stillness, as it sent both ears perking straight forward. It acted like music, but it felt more akin to the lament of a mourning dove; it had that soul to it like a thing that came from one's own blood, not from a page of sheet music. But even from where they waited, they could feel its pull. Could imagine what that woman felt with Hazzel's focus properly on her as she appeared in the window, then the door, then down the porch steps. The coyote gave another glance up at the moon, noted the faintest stain of blood just barely beginning to blur into one edge of her perfect face. Then silently rose from where they had been laying, and slithered away through the sand. She had followed Hazzel out from the immediate area of the house, nearer to where the homestead had its little shed for the goats. Those goats had bedded down for the night, blissfully ignorant of what was creeping around them--but they would be safe. Might not even have a reason to rouse at all.

Considering he'd just watched them paralyze a woman and speak soliloquies at her until they murdered her, he had no reason to disbelieve the claim. Being manhandled like this while so small wasn't a normal feeling for him, though he remained still and compliant enough to be lifted, legs dangling. Quietly he hoped it was not about to become a habit. "Mm. Men are shit at retelling stories. Shit at understanding what they're looking at, too. But no, this one's real. Both are... "Don't tell me that's sticking." "I like it. Suits you." The shadows chuckled, approval clear in the shade's voice. "Yeah, of course you do." He huffed. The act of grabbing the foot beneath their fingers suggested the answer even before the hare could open his mouth to reply - not only was the color similar to the cane but the texture was too, wood smoothly taking over as fur stopped growing. It was smoother of a transition than a proper prosthetic was, but most of that seemed visual. Fingers could feel the subtle flexing in the wood, not from muscles or tendons, but joinery allowing it movement that a cane was normally not expected (or frankly, supposed) to have. Even the little toes had their own joints. "Mostly just for show. It works a lot more like a regular foot, and I can at least tell I'm touching the ground with it, but not much else." He paused. "Not that I think you can break it. It's his anyways, I'm just borrowing it."
Daarka 3/7/2026 12:49 PM
"I ain't gonna break it," they reassured easily. "Not that I think I could, either." They grasped those little articulated "wooden" toes in their claws and gave them an experimental flex. "Better solution than a tiny lil' cane, at any rate. Nice little fit." Their gaze lifted to the antlers then, head tipping contemplatively. Woozy 3/7/2026 2:55 PM
Hazzel made an affirmative sound, grateful he couldn't really feel the moondevil playing with his tootsies like this. "I like the cane, but I'll admit this is much more convenient," he agreed. Daarka 3/7/2026 3:20 PM
They hissed a snicker as those thick kickers thumped into their wrist, and obligingly dropped the jackalope. Which, unlike their manhandling, was a very light weight that didn't threaten to overstimulate. -# It also did wonders to take the edge off the autumnal chill that had begun to creep in with the absence of the sunlight. Satisfied they had made themselves inconspicuous, the coyote planted its chin on its paws and settled in to wait however long it took for the barkeep to return home. (edited)
Woozy 3/7/2026 6:20 PM

Woozy 3/7/2026 8:25 PM

Daarka 3/7/2026 9:14 PM
Those big bunny ears just barely had the moondevil's own big ones beat for sensitivity--and they knew that well enough that when they saw them twitch and Hazzel immediately silence himself, they exercised the immense self-control to bite back what, under other circumstances, might have been a very loud bark of laughter. A thin wheeze was all that escaped before they had themselves reined back in appropriately, and turned their attention towards the approaching barkeep. Their head settled comfortably back on their paws, as if they weren't cozied in to watch one of the worst moments of the man's life unfold. There was something peculiar about such vengeance. Because, of course, it didn't make the moondevil's kin any less dead. It did nothing to right what had been made wrong. And so that lingering frustration--or maybe it was grief in the only way they knew how to experience it--rooted deep inside their chest, that didn't really get eased. There was no exhale of relief as they watched the man crumble next to ma'am's corpse (they had heard him call her name, and willfully chose to immediately forget it). But there was satisfaction. That ache was like a bug bite that this scratched just fine. You could similarly argue it wasn't the right way to handle that discomfort--but it felt good. And if the bug bite of it all wasn't going away any time soon, then they considered themselves more than entitled to an indulgent scratch. In that way, at least, something felt better. They had been toying with the barkeep plenty by now, finding only disdain for how obtuse he chose to remain about the signs he'd been given, the way the freak incidents of tragedy around town had been moving in one, unwavering direction that led to this very spot. Even if he had been paying attention and listening, the demon was still hellbent on collecting what was owed. But they would have at least respected a man who had been seeing it coming a scrap more than this sorry mess. But then again, that was where the trouble had began in the first place. A whole town with its head up its own ass. They lingered to savor it, even after the itch-scratching stopped being satisfying and bled into something more pathetic. If they were a creature more like Hazzel, someone with more of a stake in humanity than just using it as a costume, they might have been able to connect with the mourning they were witnessing. Empathize, even. They knew, conceptually, that this was the eye for an eye--they had said as much moments ago--but they related so little to the man in front of them that it only felt like they had taken a toy away from a child. A sheltered child with both their eyes intact, who ignorantly believed losing a toy was a pain similar to a gouged eye, and wept in front of an eyeless man as if it were their life that had been ruined. It finally reached a point where it was more annoying than anything else. They wanted him to wallow in this suffering--but they didn't care to be subjected to the sound of it anymore. Carefully, they shifted where they lay so they could give the jackalope a small nudge to his side, gesturing then with their nose out into the desert where they could skirt back around the property and slip away to the hut's usual spot with the least disturbance. They waited until his little white rear had gotten behind enough desert that there was no longer even line of sight to spot the white fur with, before rising to their own feet and following behind. They could have--arguably should have--just slipped neatly away like that. But instead, they deviated from the safer path away from the homestead. Still moving in the right direction, but trotting parallel to the yard, slipping in and out of being just a little too close. Caring just a little bit less about being perfectly quiet with each stride. (edited)
9:14 PM
They knew what they were doing. Coyotes didn't use knives. Didn't carve open a throat and leave every inch of flesh perfectly intact and bare of tooth marks. As far as he knew, moondevils didn't, either. They expected it'd be quite some time before the man even considered whether or not moondevils had other faces and secret lives he never learned about. So when the sun came back, they could turn the town inside out searching for a murderer. The kind you hanged. Coyotes, at least tonight, were just a mindful little harbinger of unease to haunt him--whether or not they had succeeded in getting his wife to do so as well. A thin little thread, connecting the only dots that mattered. They didn't stop moving, though. Glimpsed though they expected to be, they were soundly disappeared back into the desert long before the man had time to do anything about a solitary coyote going about its nighttime business. They kept up that easy trot the whole way back, enjoying the night--now returned to a wash of properly silver moonlight, muted in its brightness with the climax of the eclipse that still had the moon’s face stained red--slowing only when they reached the general vicinity Hazzel usually chose for the hut. But the hut wasn't their chore, so they idled to a halt, their attention outward to the desert at large. (edited)
Woozy 3/8/2026 2:20 PM
Hazzel had less of a taste for watching a man suffer at his lowest lows. He found no faults in causing it, no, but he did not derive the same kind of pleasure from the action that a demon did either. He was just the delivery guy, here to help distribute bitter bloody truths to the world. And frankly, it wasn't his itch he was here to help scratch. So when the coyote nudged him and gestured that it was time to leave, he had no complaints at silently turning and slipping out into the desert the long way around, just enough distance to allow himself the assurance that no one saw him head to their usual spot. No one except the moon, anyways... though he figured she could keep watching as long as she liked. Even when he was far enough away that he could have shifted back into his usual form and walk with a cane the rest of the way, he continued on foot as a jackalope, a white streak under bloody moonlight in the desert. Something about the small form felt comforting, feeling that feral heart pump desperately at much higher speeds than his usual human heart did, spurring him on. Maybe he just liked the way he could run on all fours instead of threes again. Even on his little legs, he reached their usual spot faster than the coyote did. He'd been running, after all, and they'd been moseying. Trotting. By the time they'd arrived he'd already gone back to his usual bipedal form, tome in hand and greeting them with that twinned whistling tune that so often accompanied his ritual casting. It was a familiar ritual, both in the literal and metaphorical sense, that was so comforting one could be forgiven for assuming nothing untoward had happened that evening at all. On the outside anyways. The smell of blood still hadn't left Hazzel's nostrils and he figured neither demon was in any hurry to forget the evening's mischief either. Once the hut was complete, he put away his tome and brushed through those gently-clattering beads to let himself in, settling down by the fire in a similar ritual to start their bedding down. Somewhere between the movement of his hands and the turn of thoughts in his head though, he finally looked their way thoughtfully. "How often you feed her like that?" He asked.
Daarka 3/8/2026 2:58 PM
The coyote’s attention stayed outward to the desert, ears swiveling thoughtfully and nose twitching at the breeze. By the time Hazzel’s question reached them, they still hadn’t come inside. “… Not often…” they replied distractedly, but any further explanation never came. After a moment, they stirred to action again, but not to join him inside. Instead, they padded off back through the desert, weaving through the brush at a steady walk. The more steps they took, the steadier their path, until they seemed to know exactly where they were headed. They didn’t go far. Didn’t need to. They’d been setting up camp in this area so consistently, their own scent had taken root on the spot—and the desert knew them by that scent in a way that didn’t have to be taught. They rounded a final shrub, and their gaze landed on a meek shape that might have been easy to overlook in the dark sand if they hadn’t known to seek it out: curled up at the roots of a small palo verde was the ragged shape of a coyote. Just one. Its pelt was unkempt and stretched over peaks where bones pressed against their skin, its body succumbed to that permeating hunger. Those discernible ribs moved still, betraying small, labored breaths. At the quiet sound of the moondevil’s paws in the sand, one eye creaked open to a dull yellow sliver, clouded in a cataract—but it didn’t manage any other movement. No energy left to even pivot its ears towards them.
Woozy 3/8/2026 3:16 PM

They hadn't said they were going anywhere, but they hadn't said they were going alone, so it took very little to urge him along after them, his quiet waltz of a gait steady on behind. Daarka 3/8/2026 3:55 PM

Woozy 3/8/2026 4:50 PM

Daarka 3/8/2026 5:16 PM
"Oh, it's a good life," they reassured easily. "Year or two longer than most. Isn't that we ain't built to go longer'n that," they added, "the life's just a hard one. Always, always hungry." They paused to give another gentle lick to her ear, smoothing over a mat they had no interest in fussing long enough to untangle. Untangling the mat wouldn't do anything but prickle and pull at her skin--but they knew how it itched the base of her ear. "She's one of the lucky ones. No mange, no infection." Their muzzle dipped slightly with a little sniff. "... A tooth or two is rottin'. Makes her gums sore, makes eatin' almost more painful at times than the starving would be. No, she one of the lucky ones. Lucky is when nothin' else got you, and all that's left is that last hunger. When you evade every other fate, that's the one you'll never outrun in the end. It's patient like that, but it never leaves survivors. If that's the one to do you in, I'd call it a good life." Daarka 3/8/2026 7:02 PM
Maybe, if they had found her a few days earlier, the little jackalope would have been tempting. Would have bought her more time. Woozy 3/8/2026 10:09 PM

Enough to make him feel like even if it had been him starving on the bloody sands under the hollow moon, he wasn't as alone as people might think. Starvation wasn't a picky eater. Daarka 3/8/2026 11:07 PM

Daarka 3/9/2026 7:33 PM
Content to leave it at that, having freed the jackalope of obligation just in case he felt any present, the moondevil gave a small dip of their chin in acknowledgement. Ran their tongue a few more times over her cheek, and then eased down to rest their head draped over her shoulder, eyes open but heavily lidded. In the quiet that fell over the small gathering, when human words were put away and the desert closed in around them more wholly, it wouldn’t be hard for Hazzel to feel perfectly folded in to the world the moondevil commonly spoke of. Wouldn’t be hard to imagine being a tawny hare without antlers, native to this desert and part of its rhythm of give and take. He was more or less the right shape, after all, to know how those hares felt the sand under their padded feet, still holding some of the day’s now-dwindling heat in their grains, and know how the sleepy hum of nighttime life fell upon those particular ears. To catch the distant hoot of an owl as more than just the background noise it was to a man, but as a directional awareness of certain danger. The owl was hungry, too. While it came with the peace of mind that some owl or snake or coyote wasn’t going to actually eat a hare who had all sorts of teeth of his own up his sleeves—and the allyship of one of the desert’s old gods, no less, who was surely not about to let him get carried off by some bird of prey—that was the kind of reasoning a human brain did. For other, more animalistic parts of him, the thrum of alertness that came with being hare-shaped in a starving desert was an inevitable whisper. But that was ultimately no different from the moondevil’s own whispers. The part of them that could perfectly see the hare-shaped creature in front of them, and had the intrusive urge to lunge—if only to excite the creature into jumping and starting a race. That whisper goaded, wagered they could get their teeth on him before he could slip out of reach if they struck just right. The desert had always loved a gamble, long before men invented cards about it. But ultimately, they were just whispers. Heard, even listened to a little, but not dominant in their mind. The moondevil stayed relaxed, not distracted by those inevitable little twinges of urges. If they were prone to succumb to them, Hazzel would already know how their teeth felt by now. It was hard to say precisely how long they sat in that vigil. Time was man’s construct, and the longer they stayed in the wordless world of the desert, the more disconnected they felt from the town a stone’s throw away and all its ways of pretending to grasp and measure reality. The moon had washed herself clean of blood and wandered partway across the sky, and the owl had flown somewhere else in the dark. Hazzel’s keen ears would hear the tiny rustle of a kangaroo rat that had daintily drawn near, only to understand what it was about to walk in on, and slip back away again. That was how much time had passed when the coyote’s labored breaths momentarily became slightly irregular. Exhale. A longer pause than usual. Deeper, almost gasping inhale. Exhale. Smaller inhale. Exhale. Then stillness. The moondevil’s head only lifted after that stillness had set over her body. They gave her face a brief sniff, running their tongue once or twice more over her cheek. Then they softly rose up again to their feet, movements unhurried but steady, neither in a rush to leave nor aching to linger. There were no last rites, no parting words, no moment of silence or forcing her eyes closed or interring her remains. Yet there was also no lack of respect and dignity afforded to her end, even as the moondevil stepped around her to pad back towards they way they had come. The closest thing they offered to a ceremony was a brief murmur for Hazzel’s benefit: “She’ll take care of her.” (edited)
Woozy 3/11/2026 12:11 PM
Hazzel let himself settle into the moment, settle into the desert as both a guest and an inhabitant, for however long it lasted. Time seemed to bleed away with every slow breath, and he let it pass through his awareness. Though he felt comfortable where he was, those little notes of animalistic instincts rose like gentle prickles, like a hand on the shoulder in reminder. The noises of the desert meant more like this, and every one around them went accounted for by the subtle shift of an ear. Daarka 3/11/2026 5:16 PM
The demon slept like a puppy after their late-night activity, predictably unfazed by the murder of the barkeep's wife--but also perfectly at ease with the coyote's passing, albeit in a far less flippant manner. Morning came warm and comfortable to the hut, despite its inhabitants being perfectly aware that news of horrendous tragedy was actively spreading through the town with the sunlight. There was no need for stress or urgency in their morning; they "didn't know yet". But when they were ready to head into town, they queued up their very best I'm so sorry for your loss faces, and rejoined society. Much like the pleasantries and other human socializations, the moondevil expected and was not surprised to find that it all came seamlessly to Hazzel. They put on a very convincing show, themselves--appropriately somber, realistically surprised, void of anything that would conceivably be seen as suspicious--and they didn't worry for a moment that it hadn't done the trick. But it was a dedicated effort, a distinctly unnatural performance they had learned, and they had a hard time believing the same was true for Hazzel with how effortless he made it look. But inorganic as the behavior comparatively was for the moondevil, they were, above all else, a creature that lied as they breathed. A clever deceit didn't always need to successfully persuade of a falsehood; sometimes all it took was a mundanity so unnoteworthy that the falsehood snuck by without scrutiny. The Thorn Jubilation knew, while polite and inoffensive, had never made himself known to be the sort offering up his own shoulder for a stranger to cry on. That part of the performance remained comfortably segregated to Hazzel's domain; all the while, the moondevil entertained themselves with realizing how far they could get merely coasting in his wake. It didn't matter if they were the one tearing up or patting a hand or rubbing a shoulder, after all. If they were there with Hazzel as he did it, aligning themselves with his words and condolences... then the total sum of the gestures came from both of them, in the mind of the recipient. They wondered, at some point amid that charade, if that was how Hazzel had felt watching them proclaim themselves a diplomat, and then go off to be welcomed into a pack of coyotes. Between the two of the diplomats they made, the whole of Talmas was naught but an open door. The barkeep's wife had an open-casket funeral ceremony. The moondevil, grumbling, surrendered a few coins to Hazzel with that; they had been sure her open throat would prevent that, but apparently it was nothing a well-placed ribbon couldn't fix, like a bandaid over--well. Over a giant, gaping wound. The barkeep himself, of course, was terribly distraught. No doubt, it would tug on the heartstrings of any who had strings there to tug in the first place. While the moondevil had quickly developed incredibly staunch faith in their new companion, it still proved to be an interesting little test, a little push at the envelope of his constitution. They could tell he wasn't immune to the tragedy, but they still found themselves impressed with how easily he could toss that feeling aside when it was time to move on. They never saw a stitch of creeping regret cross the man's face. He did not wring his hands and express newly-cold feet about the work left ahead. The demon watched for those things. Expected them, even. But when they never came, when his empathy never slowed either of them down or got in the way, they found they had no trouble at all forgiving that bleeding heart, nor holding it against him. They suspected they were doing nothing but walking the path Fable had already cleared long ago.
5:16 PM
The barkeep took some time off work to grieve. That was just as well; he hadn't been the only hunter. Far from it. The span of two whole weeks sprawled ahead of them before the new moon, and the moondevil didn't waste those days. Tragedy had been stalking homes and loved ones under the cloak of night, never giving the residents time to adjust to the manner of threat before it had already escalated, leaving the town terminally off-balance. And now, in the wake of the murder, the daylight ceased to be sufficient to ward off misfortune. They didn't often strike the same note twice. A gate was left open and a herd of cattle wandered loose, prompting a frazzled round-up effort. Some properties were vandalized, windows shattered even when people were present inside--but never with a culprit there when the man of the house rushed out to see what had happened. A great deal of coin kept going missing. There were three snake bites in one day, two of them proving fatal. Some horses would occasionally become inconsolably spooked for long periods, as if beset by vengeful ghosts. Fires had a way of jumping from their confines with an unnatural volatility, catching a curtain or awning once or twice and causing a commotion before they could be put out. Sigils appeared in the dirt in strange but conspicuous locations, and even the arcanely-gifted among the townsfolk weren't sure what they spelled out, or what language they represented. A farmer helped his cow deliver her calf, and in the mere seconds he had turned away to wipe clean his hands of the afterbirth, the calf simply vanished without a trace, just a few feet away from the farmer. He looked for it for hours to no avail before giving up and spending his night sleeplessly disturbed. The next morning, the calf had reappeared exactly where he last saw it--still alive, even--but with no trace of its gouged-out eyes. It was stressed and frail as a result of being kept from nursing, and its mother had been bellowing in distress trying to reach it. The farmer, at the advice of the priest, shot it. It wasn't until a child was shot in the head in broad daylight where he had been playing in his own yard that the town truly snapped. Not just any child, of course: the butcher's young son. The butcher who had offered to contribute his butchering skills to the demon-hunting adventures. Whose own knife had been the one to cleave free those coyote-shaped heads stained in bitter ichor. Who had lightened the mood with sarcastic remarks about the nature of the demons' gore, even as the acrid smell of it raked at his senses like a final thrash of claws. Oh, they'd heard him. By the time he and his wife ran out into the yard after the sound of the gunshot, that same crow was perched on the roof--and he heard him just as clearly as he wailed and shouted and his wife screamed. As they whirled looking for who could have done such a thing, and saw only an abandoned revolver lying in the dirt. The neighbor's revolver. All hell broke loose. It didn't matter how well they'd known that neighbor. Everything was called into fiery question in an instant. The neighbor's self-preservation reared up to defend them, and they did the only thing they could to absolve themselves of blame: they passed the buck. A crowd had gathered at the sounds of the gunshot and screaming and now furious, bellowing accusations right out in the yard for all to hear--so all did hear as the neighbor implicated the man down the street, in the house with the brittle bush. He had confided to them that he had seemingly miraculously accrued all of the wealth from the nearby houses--and he was now surely regretting sharing that information and sought to frame the neighbor, to do away with a pair of loose lips armed with incriminating words. 5:16 PM
The raging father walked the neighbor at gunpoint down the street, the neighbor's life seemingly hinging upon the truthfulness of the claim. The gathering crowd followed. Someone might have run to fetch the sheriff, but vigilante justice was a more rapid affair than that would be. The father was not without friends in that crowd, friends who were duly horrified to hear him scream that his son was dead, who's shock and horror coalesced nicely into the shape of a small mob. One that nearly broke down the door of the brittle bush house, forced its way inside, and ransacked the place until someone yelled out that they'd found it. The neighbor evaded the muzzle of the father's shotgun, and the mob dragged the brittle bush residents--husband and wife both--out to the street in a blur of chaotic violence. The man's ribs ran into someone's fist a few times until they cracked. Their windfall of Luck had soured and curdled irreversibly by the time the sheriff showed up to feign some semblance of due process, slapping iron shackles onto the couple to drag them to the jailhouse. The moondevil got their coin back from Hazzel. In a just world, a court's trial proceedings would suffice to determine guilt from innocence. The innocent would have nothing to fear, because the truth would be found for what it was. But how in the fuck did one explain that the sum of their neighbors’ wealth had magically appeared in their home one day, while maintaining they had nothing to do with it? There was a hopelessness in their eyes as they plead their case, hearing the words even as they left their own mouths. They did what any good Jubilant would do: blamed the devil and begged mercy from god. But the devil didn't scheme so tangibly in a town like this. The devil lived in impure thoughts and bad habits, not random strokes of impossibility. Something about claiming otherwise had a way of calling into question their plea to god, too. They met the gallows a mere day later, the whole affair a whirlwind of catastrophe. The moondevil saved Hazzel a spot to watch. Two necks snapped in unison, and the whole town tangibly took a misguided breath of air, needing to believe evil had been put to rest and the tailspin of tragedy was quelled. The rest of the day within Jubilation was a somber and sore one, like the whole town was favoring an injured limb. The little boy's body was handed over to the undertaker, his funeral set for the following day. The sun finally set--but the moon slipped into the sky unnoticed, drowned fully in a blanket of shadow. "Ever dabbled in burglary?" They rasped in lilting greeting. (edited)

Daarka 3/16/2026 1:16 PM
Hazzel had been right to relocate himself at top speeds, because it turned out the moondevil was amped up enough to have a twitchy trigger finger. The jackalope had only just fallen still in his chosen spot when any worries about the view were deafeningly assuaged as his senses would be momentarily bludgeoned with an enormous, sickly green explosion that momentarily seemed to swallow the entirety of the visible world. The force of the blast not only rattled the ground beneath those fur-lined feet, but the wave of dry, crackling heat was almost enough to send the little creature staggering back a pace or two; any larger a form and he might have been laid flat on his ass. But as his brain caught up to what his eyes and ears had been assaulted with, he'd process what had been the sound of shattering glass, every window in the tavern and general store blown clean open, several cracks of unidentified bits of wood buckling under the blast--and an unnatural flame swallowing the entirety of the conjoined buildings in a roaring, ravenous eruption of dizzying light. The gunpowder would have been explosive, but not this explosive, almost threatening to strip the buildings' walls clean off their foundations in an instant. Then again, fire should have been orange. Should have given off billows of smoke that would have been blooming into a swollen haze overhead by now. Fire was indiscriminately destructive. Volatile, at times--but a neutral entity. This fire discriminated--the neighboring structures suffered some damage in the force of the explosion, but notably, none of the flames found purchase to conflagrate upon them. This fire knew hatred. If the property damage was anything to go off of, it was safe to assume being much nearer than Hazzel was would have been a serious safety concern. To be at the epicenter of the blast? A death sentence. But if Hazzel had any lingering confusion or uncertainties about the moondevils' relationship with fire, they were likely answered when a streak of smoldering green neatly, almost calmly, leapt through one of the now-windowless holes in the wall. The shape of the burning coyote landed soundly on the ground again, and with a little shake of their pelt, the flames that had comfortably swallowed them were loosed free once more, wisping away into the rest of the night's catastrophe. In their wake, the coyote's pelt was unmarred--void of even soot stains--as if the violent, hateful fire had been nothing more than a stirring breeze through their fur. They spared no glance back, springing into a thoroughly carefree lope towards where Hazzel waited with their tail waving aloft behind them. They didn't give a fat shit if they were seen in that moment. If the locals clocked a coyote leaving the scene of the crime. Good. They skidded to a halt in the dirt by the jackalope, teeth partially bared in a sharp grin that a normal coyote did not make as they turned to watch the blaze steadily demolish Jubilation's little semblance of a tower of Babylon. By then, the shouts of men were just starting to become audible as the commotion drew people into the street, then into a panic to try to contain and put out the inferno. The coyote settled onto their haunches, not bothering to position themselves for ensured subtlety. As the awareness of the disaster and the bustle of panic grew, it wasn't long before--even from their position--they could glimpse the efforts of the horrified locals. The neighboring buildings were fussed at to try to prevent the fire's spread, only to realize no amount of the fire touching those buildings was causing them to catch. Buckets of water were fetched and thrown onto the blaze... only to watch the eerie conflagration swallow that water like it meant nothing. No hiss of drowning cinders, no plume of smoke. The compounded horror of this realization gradually became noticeable even from their distance. The coyote's unnatural grin widened. (edited)
1:16 PM

“Well lucky you; you gettin’ both.” They slowed to a halt, reaching into what was decidedly some sort of extradimensional pocket beneath their poncho, gently pulling forth one large, canid skull streaked in stubborn, black gore. “They never belonged here. But we knew, that day, why we had been taught to lie. We knew it in the way their axes cleaved through the palo verde and left them wounds weepin’. When that, too, eased back to calm, the flesh and blood survivor sat—almost sagging in a tangible feeling of relief—on their haunches, surrounded by the specters of their kin still snuffling and nuzzling and licking at one another. After a while, the yellow gaze turned to find Hazzel and Fable again, their presence not forgotten in the background. One by one, skeletal eye sockets turned to follow that gaze with their own, acknowledging their presence for the first time.

Eventually man-brain felt quite comfortable enough (Echo's comfort and lack of hurry was about as good an indicator as any) to reach out and run an experimental hand along one of those flickering forms, fingers looking to dig in and give a little scratch. Fable remained where he was as a sheet of darkness their flames could shine off of, though their approaching snouts earned a contemplative one from him as well. A sort of animalistic greeting that required no words before the shadows settled, satisfied with the group's newly acquired companions. Hazzel's attention turned back to Echo at their words, giving them a smile with just how familiar those words seemed to be in spirit. "Well now ain't that just the beauty of it? You got all the time in the world now. Got a thousand and one tomorrow's to think about, to ignore, to wake and greet each morning with the sun's rising."

The one born first—would’a been my sister back then—was the best of us with mirages. They could make you believe anything. Could manifest entire realities just for your eyes alone. Sight, sound, smell, taste, touch, all of it. If you hallucinate an animal bitin’ you, how your mind supposed to reckon with it when the dog is gone but you still got a fresh bite wound? There was a soul in their falsehoods that we loved watchin’ drive men mad. The one born after—a brother—worked real well with ‘em, ‘cause they got the stickiest fingers I ever known. Ain’t a pocket they couldn’t get into. Ain’t a door they couldn’t unlock, or a room they couldn’t enter. Not like I do, either—they do it in their own body. The places I can only break into if I’m wearin’ a spider’s skin, they’d stroll right in at their full height. And do it faster than me, too. Real keen eye on ‘em. When men started puttin’ out traps for us, it was them that found most of ‘em.” They flashed a toothy grin at Hazzel. “Kept us from lookin’ like you.” They looked around again, drawing in a deep breath, and exhaling as they got eyes on one of the larger specters. “And that was my other sister. A force of nature in every sense of the word. The way you walk around town and stick your nose in everyone’s business, rememberin’ all their damn names and stories—that was them with the wind, the rain, the sand and sun. Them neighbors bended and danced to their whims. Their song could call a thunderstorm in the middle of a summer day. Knew fire on a first-name basis, too. It was their idea to put it in man’s blood. It was their skill that placed it there. It was their call that fire answered when it was time to collect our dues. You only know me, only seen what I can do. Without seein’ what they can do, you ain’t know a damn thing about how afraid of us they all should have stayed.” A beat. “I should know, ‘cause the damn fucker chased me off my food for the first couple years and had it all to themselves. That’s why they’re so fat n—” They did not finish that thought before the ghost barreled into them like a spectral freight train, sending them sprawling on their back with a winded but unapologetic cackling wheeze.
Woozy 4/26/2026 9:08 PM
Though Hazzel's heckle had been a tease he knew full well would get turned over faster than a flower pot, he still settled in comfortably to witness just how Echo described the ghosts around them. The first would be the easiest for him to remember, to really remember, with that cracked hole in the skull as a very easy visual... the rest would come with time. So instead he drank every word of their description, eyes taking in the way the clothes seemed to fuse to their skin in a skin-deep facsimile of humanity. Not like they ever needed anything more than that. Save the poncho, he reckoned with some quiet personal amusement. (Not that he was complaining about their fashion choices, of course). There was something about the way they described their family that had a smile rest comfortably on his lips, gaze flicking helplessly between the unfamiliar shades, nodding along as his memory instead did the hard work of remembering who they were. What they did, down to every lovingly described crime. It rang a little bell of homesickness deep inside him, and it was one he was content to let ring and surround himself with a new family to grow old with. Kept us from lookin' like you earned a laugh, even with the joke being made at his own expense. He laughed even harder when the ghost barreled them over for the offense, grateful to be holding onto the stool he'd propped his elbows up to lean on for storytime. Not that, seated as he was, he was really going to be going anywhere even with as heavy of a laugh as he had. Instead he simply shook the wood into little creaks, a skinny fist pounding lightly on the seat a couple times in a desperate attempt to reel himself back in. He wiped a tear from his eyes, flashing a toothy grin of his own back their way. "Sounds like you all had plenty of time to get all sorts of talents sharpened and primed up. Hoowee. Would that I'd shown up sooner 'n got to see you all in your prime."

Daarka 4/26/2026 9:34 PM
In a human vessel like this, Echo didn’t exactly have the tools to wrestle properly with the fiery skull whose teeth had clamped hazardously over their face and neck. Frankly, a little too reminiscent of the tussle they had gotten in minutes prior; Hazzel wouldn’t be misguided for assuming the sister had probably been the same one going for his cane. But despite having their head in the jaws of certain death, they were still cackling with the kind of glee universally found in annoying little siblings who got their licks in where they could. The chaos only lasted a similarly short moment as it had before, before Echo’s thrashing around went resignedly limp, and the jaws were released from their face to drift away into semi-corporeality again. They still had a grin on their face, though, lifting an arm to scuff their sleeve over where the ghost-teeth had reddened their skin as they sat back up. “Nah, we woulda ate you.” A beat, and they paused to actually think about that. “Well. No. You had Fable by the time you got here.” They tipped their head back and forth contemplatively. “Whole pack’a moondevils a fair bit harder to negotiate with than one starvin’ one. Put his silver tongue to the test proper. But reckon he’d have managed. Think even at our cagiest, we still got a soft spot for shadows with good ideas. Don’t know what we’d do with you after that, though.” (edited)
Woozy 5/28/2026 11:43 AM
Their immediate claim towards eating him was an expected one, though it still earned a laugh that was probably not the correct response to such a comment were he anyone else. The walk back towards Fable’s involvement had him cast a knowing smirk the shade’s way, who grinned in return. “Oh, I think we would’ve found some fun games to play either way,” Fable purred, though the grin faded into a more simple shrug of the shadows. “Would’ve had our fun, but I think we would’ve carried on as always after a while. Swing by like a wayward wind to stir up trouble, then leave once we’d had our fun.” Hazzel hummed, then split another grin. “Could always take you with us. How you feel about a trip out to the Kurul Taiga? I hear the ice melts sometimes in the summer, if you’re lucky.” The expression on his face was already anticipating the answer.

Still… something about the tease on the heels of what Fable had said gave them a bit of pause. A beat to recognize how Hazzel had never really explicitly said he wasn’t planning to leave again. “The fuck you want outta some frigid wasteland, anyways?”
